What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… my regular doctor thought it would be a good idea for me to come in. She’s been concerned because my weight’s gotten pretty low, lower than she likes, I guess. Everyone’s sort of making a big fuss about it.\n\nI don’t feel like it’s as bad as they think, honestly. Being lighter kind of helps me keep moving at the shop, you know? Carpentry is physical, and I don’t like feeling slowed down. But… she wanted me to talk to someone, so I’m here. Is that okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount or types of food you eat?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t say I’m trying to be difficult about food. On days I’m in the workshop, I usually just have tea, and maybe a few careful bites of something if I feel I should. Stopping for a whole meal feels… unnecessary, I guess, and it kind of throws off the control I have over the day. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight if you eat more?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, a bit. I don’t like saying it that way because it sounds vain or silly, and I know you’re not trying to make me feel judged.\n\nBut if I eat something heavy, or those rich holiday foods we used to make, I start worrying pretty quickly that I’ll gain weight. It feels like I’d lose the grip I’ve worked hard to keep, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently lower than a healthy weight for someone of your height and build?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really think so, no. I know my husband says I look too thin, and the apprentices at the shop have made comments too, which is a bit embarrassing.\n\nBut I keep telling them I’m fine. I think sometimes people see discipline and they call it sickness, you know? Maybe I’m on the lighter side, but I don’t see it the way they do. They’re all making quite a fuss over the number.\n\nBut… when I think about it myself, I don’t really think of it as lower than ideal. I suppose I feel like being lighter helps me keep up at the shop and not feel slowed down. I know that may not be the answer you’re looking for, but that’s honestly how it feels to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant weight loss or gain over the past year?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, my weight has gone down. That’s really why my doctor sent me here, because it’s gotten low enough that everyone is making quite a fuss about it.\n\nI don’t mean to sound dismissive. I know they’re concerned. It’s just that, from my side, being this light feels like it helps me keep up at the shop and not feel slowed down.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… maybe the control part is a little stronger on workdays, if I’m honest.

On days I’m in the workshop, I usually just have tea, and maybe a few careful bites of something. It doesn’t feel like I’m starving myself exactly… it’s more that stopping for a proper meal feels unnecessary, and it throws off the order I’ve got going for the day.

So yes, I suppose when I’m deciding how much to eat, keeping that routine steady can matter more than the actual fear of gaining weight. Is that… the sort of thing you mean?

She’s been concerned because my weight’s gotten pretty low, lower than she likes, I guess. Everyone’s sort of making a big fuss about it.\n\nI don’t feel like it’s as bad as they think, honestly. Being lighter kind of helps me keep moving at the shop, you know? Carpentry is physical, and I don’t like feeling slowed down. But… she wanted me to talk to someone, so I’m here. Is that okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount or types of food you eat?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t say I’m trying to be difficult about food. On days I’m in the workshop, I usually just have tea, and maybe a few careful bites of something if I feel I should. Stopping for a whole meal feels… unnecessary, I guess, and it kind of throws off the control I have over the day. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight if you eat more?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, a bit. I don’t like saying it that way because it sounds vain or silly, and I know you’re not trying to make me feel judged.\n\nBut if I eat something heavy, or those rich holiday foods we used to make, I start worrying pretty quickly that I’ll gain weight. It feels like I’d lose the grip I’ve worked hard to keep, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently lower than a healthy weight for someone of your height and build?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really think so, no. I know my husband says I look too thin, and the apprentices at the shop have made comments too, which is a bit embarrassing.\n\nBut I keep telling them I’m fine. I think sometimes people see discipline and they call it sickness, you know? Maybe I’m on the lighter side, but I don’t see it the way they do. Is that… does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your menstrual cycle?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can really answer that in a useful way. I haven’t noticed anything I could clearly point to, if that’s what you mean. At my age it’s… not something I track the way I might have years ago.\n\nIs that okay to say? I don’t want to give you the wrong impression.\n\nDoctor: Do you make sure to exercise or be physically active even when you're not working in the carpentry shop?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I “make sure” to, exactly. The shop keeps me moving quite a lot already, so most days I feel like that counts.\n\nIf I’m not working, I might still do little things around the house or take a walk, but I don’t know that I’d call it exercise in the serious sense. I try not to be lazy, I suppose. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to limit food intake more about avoiding weight gain or about feeling in control of your daily routine?\n\nPatient: I suppose… if I’m being honest, it’s more about keeping the day orderly. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… my regular doctor thought it would be a good idea for me to come in. She’s been concerned because my weight’s gotten pretty low, lower than she likes, I guess. Everyone’s sort of making a big fuss about it.\n\nI don’t feel like it’s as bad as they think, honestly. Being lighter kind of helps me keep moving at the shop, you know? Carpentry is physical, and I don’t like feeling slowed down. But… she wanted me to talk to someone, so I’m here. Is that okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount or types of food you eat?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t say I’m trying to be difficult about food. On days I’m in the workshop, I usually just have tea, and maybe a few careful bites of something if I feel I should. Stopping for a whole meal feels… unnecessary, I guess, and it kind of throws off the control I have over the day. I don’t like saying it that way because it sounds vain or silly, and I know you’re not trying to make me feel judged.\n\nBut if I eat something heavy, or those rich holiday foods we used to make, I start worrying pretty quickly that I’ll gain weight. It feels like I’d lose the grip I’ve worked hard to keep, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently lower than a healthy weight for someone of your height and build?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really think so, no. I know my husband says I look too thin, and the apprentices at the shop have made comments too, which is a bit embarrassing.\n\nBut I keep telling them I’m fine. I think sometimes people see discipline and they call it sickness, you know? Maybe I’m on the lighter side, but I don’t see it the way they do. Is that… does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your menstrual cycle?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can really answer that in a useful way. I haven’t noticed anything I could clearly point to, if that’s what you mean. At my age it’s… not something I track the way I might have years ago.\n\nIs that okay to say? I don’t want to give you the wrong impression.\n\nDoctor: Do you make sure to exercise or be physically active even when you're not working in the carpentry shop?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I “make sure” to, exactly. The shop keeps me moving quite a lot already, so most days I feel like that counts.\n\nIf I’m not working, I might still do little things around the house or take a walk, but I don’t know that I’d call it exercise in the serious sense. I try not to be lazy, I suppose. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to limit food intake more about avoiding weight gain or about feeling in control of your daily routine?\n\nPatient: I suppose… if I’m being honest, it’s more about keeping the day orderly. On workshop days I usually just have tea, and maybe a few careful bites of something, because stopping for a proper meal feels unnecessary and sort of throws everything off.\n\nI don’t mean that to sound extreme. It just feels easier that way, like I’ve got control of the day. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: When you think about eating more or having a larger meal, what is the first thing that comes to mind?\n\nPatient: I guess the first thing is… that it would feel like too much. Not dangerous exactly, I don’t want to overstate it, but if it’s something heavy, or those holiday foods we used to make, my mind goes straight to, “What if this makes me gain weight?”\n\nAnd then I worry I’ll lose the grip I’ve worked pretty hard to keep. I know that may sound a bit rigid. I’m trying to answer honestly.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you limit your food intake?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, at least a big part of it is. I don’t like admitting that, because I know it probably sounds silly at my age, but if I eat anything heavy, or let myself have the holiday foods we used to make, I start worrying right away that I’ll gain weight.\n\nAnd then it’s not just the weight, it’s this feeling like I’ll lose the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. That’s really why my doctor sent me here, because it’s gotten low enough that everyone is making quite a fuss about it.\n\nI don’t mean to sound dismissive. I know they’re concerned. It’s just that, from my side, being this light feels like it helps me keep up at the shop and not feel slowed down. But yes… there has been weight loss.\n\nDoctor: Is your desire to maintain control over your daily routine more important to you than your fear of gaining weight when deciding how much to eat?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe the control part is a little stronger on workdays, if I’m honest.\n\nOn days I’m in the workshop, I usually just have tea, and maybe a few careful bites of something. It doesn’t feel like I’m starving myself exactly… it’s more that stopping for a proper meal feels unnecessary, and it throws off the order I’ve got going for the day.\n\nSo yes, I suppose when I’m deciding how much to eat, keeping that routine steady can matter more than the actual fear of gaining weight. Is that… the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you decide to limit your food intake, is it more because you're afraid of gaining weight or because you want to feel in control of your body?\n\nPatient: I think it’s… more about feeling in control, if I’m being honest. Not that the weight part isn’t in there somewhere, but on workshop days especially, I get into a rhythm.\n\nI’ll usually just have tea, maybe a few careful bites of something, and it feels manageable that way. Stopping for a proper meal feels like it breaks the day up too much, like I lose the control I’ve got over things. I know that may sound strange… but that’s how it feels to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. On workshop days I usually just have tea, and maybe a few careful bites of something, because stopping for a proper meal feels unnecessary and sort of throws everything off.\n\nI don’t mean that to sound extreme. It just feels easier that way, like I’ve got control of the day. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: When you think about eating more or having a larger meal, what is the first thing that comes to mind?\n\nPatient: I guess the first thing is… that it would feel like too much. Not dangerous exactly, I don’t want to overstate it, but if it’s something heavy, or those holiday foods we used to make, my mind goes straight to, “What if this makes me gain weight?”\n\nAnd then I worry I’ll lose the grip I’ve worked pretty hard to keep. I know that may sound a bit rigid. I’m trying to answer honestly.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you limit your food intake?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, at least a big part of it is. I don’t like admitting that, because I know it probably sounds silly at my age, but if I eat anything heavy, or let myself have the holiday foods we used to make, I start worrying right away that I’ll gain weight.\n\nAnd then it’s not just the weight, it’s this feeling like I’ll lose the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. So yes… I suppose that fear is there more than I’d like to say. But yes… there has been weight loss.\n\nDoctor: Is your desire to maintain control over your daily routine more important to you than your fear of gaining weight when deciding how much to eat?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe the control part is a little stronger on workdays, if I’m honest.\n\nOn days I’m in the workshop, I usually just have tea, and maybe a few careful bites of something. I’m trying to answer you honestly.\n\nDoctor: When you restrict your food intake, is it primarily because you are afraid of gaining weight or because you want to maintain a sense of control over your life?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s both, but… if I’m being completely honest, the fear of gaining weight is a big part of it.\n\nI try not to make it sound dramatic, because I know people eat those things and they’re fine, but if I eat anything heavy, or let myself have the foods we used to make around holidays, I start worrying right away that I’ll gain weight. Then it feels like I’m losing the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep.\n\nDoes that answer it properly?\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating pattern more about maintaining control over your daily life or about avoiding weight gain?\n\nPatient: I think… I want to say it’s about control, because that sounds a little more reasonable somehow. I know that may sound a bit rigid. I’m trying to answer honestly.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you limit your food intake?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, at least a big part of it is. I don’t like admitting that, because I know it probably sounds silly at my age, but if I eat anything heavy, or let myself have the holiday foods we used to make, I start worrying right away that I’ll gain weight.\n\nAnd then it’s not just the weight, it’s this feeling like I’ll lose the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. So yes… I suppose that fear is there more than I’d like to say. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: When you think about your weight, do you think it's lower than what you consider ideal for yourself?\n\nPatient: I know everyone keeps saying it’s too low — my doctor sent me here because of that, really. They’re all making quite a fuss over the number.\n\nBut… when I think about it myself, I don’t really think of it as lower than ideal. I suppose I feel like being lighter helps me keep up at the shop and not feel slowed down. I know that may not be the answer you’re looking for, but that’s honestly how it feels to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant weight loss or gain over the past year?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, my weight has gone down. That’s really why my doctor sent me here, because it’s gotten low enough that everyone is making quite a fuss about it.\n\nI don’t mean to sound dismissive. I know they’re concerned. It’s just that, from my side, being this light feels like it helps me keep up at the shop and not feel slowed down. But yes… there has been weight loss.\n\nDoctor: Is your desire to maintain control over your daily routine more important to you than your fear of gaining weight when deciding how much to eat?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe the control part is a little stronger on workdays, if I’m honest.\n\nOn days I’m in the workshop, I usually just have tea, and maybe a few careful bites of something. It doesn’t feel like I’m starving myself exactly… it’s more that stopping for a proper meal feels unnecessary, and it throws off the order I’ve got going for the day.\n\nSo yes, I suppose when I’m deciding how much to eat, keeping that routine steady can matter more than the actual fear of gaining weight. Is that… the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you decide to limit your food intake, is it more because you're afraid of gaining weight or because you want to feel in control of your body?\n\nPatient: I think it’s… more about feeling in control, if I’m being honest. Not that the weight part isn’t in there somewhere, but on workshop days especially, I get into a rhythm.\n\nI’ll usually just have tea, maybe a few careful bites of something, and it feels manageable that way. Stopping for a proper meal feels like it breaks the day up too much, like I lose the control I’ve got over things. I’m trying to answer you honestly.\n\nDoctor: When you restrict your food intake, is it primarily because you are afraid of gaining weight or because you want to maintain a sense of control over your life?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s both, but… if I’m being completely honest, the fear of gaining weight is a big part of it.\n\nI try not to make it sound dramatic, because I know people eat those things and they’re fine, but if I eat anything heavy, or let myself have the foods we used to make around holidays, I start worrying right away that I’ll gain weight. And then it feels like I’m losing the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep.\n\nI hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to restrict your food intake if you didn't worry about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think I wouldn’t… that it’s more about routine or discipline or something like that. On days I’m in the workshop, I usually just have tea, and maybe a few careful bites of something if I feel I should. Stopping for a whole meal feels… unnecessary, I guess, and it kind of throws off the control I have over the day. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight if you eat more?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, a bit. I don’t like saying it that way because it sounds vain or silly, and I know you’re not trying to make me feel judged.\n\nBut if I eat something heavy, or those rich holiday foods we used to make, I start worrying pretty quickly that I’ll gain weight. It feels like I’d lose the grip I’ve worked hard to keep, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently lower than a healthy weight for someone of your height and build?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really think so, no. I know my husband says I look too thin, and the apprentices at the shop have made comments too, which is a bit embarrassing.\n\nBut I keep telling them I’m fine. I think sometimes people see discipline and they call it sickness, you know? Maybe I’m on the lighter side, but I don’t see it the way they do. Is that… does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your menstrual cycle?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can really answer that in a useful way. I haven’t noticed anything I could clearly point to, if that’s what you mean. At my age it’s… not something I track the way I might have years ago.\n\nIs that okay to say? I don’t want to give you the wrong impression.\n\nDoctor: Do you make sure to exercise or be physically active even when you're not working in the carpentry shop?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I “make sure” to, exactly. The shop keeps me moving quite a lot already, so most days I feel like that counts.\n\nIf I’m not working, I might still do little things around the house or take a walk, but I don’t know that I’d call it exercise in the serious sense. I try not to be lazy, I suppose. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to limit food intake more about avoiding weight gain or about feeling in control of your daily routine?\n\nPatient: I suppose… if I’m being honest, it’s more about keeping the day orderly. On workshop days I usually just have tea, and maybe a few careful bites of something, because stopping for a proper meal feels unnecessary and sort of throws everything off.\n\nI don’t mean that to sound extreme. It just feels easier that way, like I’ve got control of the day. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: When you think about eating more or having a larger meal, what is the first thing that comes to mind?\n\nPatient: I guess the first thing is… that it would feel like too much. Not dangerous exactly, I don’t want to overstate it, but if it’s something heavy, or those holiday foods we used to make, my mind goes straight to, “What if this makes me gain weight?”\n\nAnd then I worry I’ll lose the grip I’ve worked pretty hard to keep. I know that may sound a bit rigid. I’m trying to answer honestly.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you limit your food intake?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, at least a big part of it is. I don’t like admitting that, because I know it probably sounds silly at my age, but if I eat anything heavy, or let myself have the holiday foods we used to make, I start worrying right away that I’ll gain weight.\n\nAnd then it’s not just the weight, it’s this feeling like I’ll lose the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. So yes… I suppose that fear is there more than I’d like to say. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: When you think about your weight, do you think it's lower than what you consider ideal for yourself?\n\nPatient: I know everyone keeps saying it’s too low — my doctor sent me here because of that, really. They’re all making quite a fuss over the number.\n\nBut… when I think about it myself, I don’t really think of it as lower than ideal. I suppose I feel like being lighter helps me keep up at the shop and not feel slowed down. I know that may not be the answer you’re looking for, but that’s honestly how it feels to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant weight loss or gain over the past year?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, my weight has gone down. That’s really why my doctor sent me here, because it’s gotten low enough that everyone is making quite a fuss about it.\n\nI don’t mean to sound dismissive. I know they’re concerned. It’s just that, from my side, being this light feels like it helps me keep up at the shop and not feel slowed down. But yes… there has been weight loss.\n\nDoctor: Is your desire to maintain control over your daily routine more important to you than your fear of gaining weight when deciding how much to eat?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe the control part is a little stronger on workdays, if I’m honest.\n\nOn days I’m in the workshop, I usually just have tea, and maybe a few careful bites of something. It doesn’t feel like I’m starving myself exactly… it’s more that stopping for a proper meal feels unnecessary, and it throws off the order I’ve got going for the day.\n\nSo yes, I suppose when I’m deciding how much to eat, keeping that routine steady can matter more than the actual fear of gaining weight. Is that… the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you decide to limit your food intake, is it more because you're afraid of gaining weight or because you want to feel in control of your body?\n\nPatient: I think it’s… more about feeling in control, if I’m being honest. Not that the weight part isn’t in there somewhere, but on workshop days especially, I get into a rhythm.\n\nI’ll usually just have tea, maybe a few careful bites of something, and it feels manageable that way. Stopping for a proper meal feels like it breaks the day up too much, like I lose the control I’ve got over things. I know that may sound strange… but that’s how it feels to me.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: It isn’t the only reason, I don’t think… but yes, it is there more than I like to admit.\n\nIf I eat something heavy, or if I let myself have the kinds of foods we used to make around holidays, I start worrying almost right away that I’ll gain weight. And then it feels like I’m losing the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep.\n\nI know that probably sounds… rigid. I’m trying to answer you honestly.\n\nDoctor: When you restrict your food intake, is it primarily because you are afraid of gaining weight or because you want to maintain a sense of control over your life?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s both, but… if I’m being completely honest, the fear of gaining weight is a big part of it.\n\nI try not to make it sound dramatic, because I know people eat those things and they’re fine, but if I eat anything heavy, or let myself have the foods we used to make around holidays, I start worrying right away that I’ll gain weight. Then it feels like I’m losing the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep.\n\nDoes that answer it properly?\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating pattern more about maintaining control over your daily life or about avoiding weight gain?\n\nPatient: I think… I want to say it’s about control, because that sounds a little more reasonable somehow. But the weight part is really tied up in it.\n\nIf I eat anything heavy, or let myself have those holiday foods we used to make, I start worrying almost immediately that I’ll gain weight. And then it feels like I’m losing the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep.\n\nI hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to restrict your food intake if you didn't worry about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think I wouldn’t… that it’s more about routine or discipline or something like that. But honestly, if the worry about my weight or shape wasn’t there, I don’t know that I’d feel the same need to restrict.\n\nIt’s when I eat something heavy, or those holiday foods we used to make, that I start panicking a bit that I’ll gain weight. Then it feels like I’m losing the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep.\n\nI’m sorry, I know I’ve said that a few times. I’m just trying to be clear.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your restrictive eating is more about avoiding weight gain or about maintaining a sense of control and routine in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s more about avoiding weight gain, if I’m being honest. The control and routine part is there, but it starts with the fear.\n\nWhen I eat something heavy, or let myself have holiday foods we used to make, I start worrying right away that I’ll gain weight. Then it feels like I’m losing the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but… yes, I think the weight gain fear is the bigger part.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight the primary reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is. I don’t really like saying that, because it sounds vain or silly, maybe, but it’s true.\n\nIf I eat something heavy, or have the holiday foods we used to make, I start worrying almost right away that I’ll gain weight. And then it feels like I’m losing that grip I’ve worked so hard to keep.\n\nSo… yes, I think that fear is the main reason.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
